```
# Initial Context
The following is the User-Provided Initial Context for this Linux kernel crash analysis. It includes the items listed in the User-Provided Data Inventory below and should be treated as already-available analysis input.

**CRITICAL**: These information blocks and command outputs are already provided below by the user. **DO NOT** attempt to request this data or run these base commands (`sys`, `sys -t`, `bt`) again at ANY step of the analysis.

**[User-Provided Data Inventory]**
1. **`sys`**: System info (kernel version, panic string, CPU count).
2. **`sys -t`**: Kernel taint flags.
3. **`bt`**: Panic task backtrace.
4. **`vmcore-dmesg`**: **IMPORTANT** - This is a text content block embedded in the User-Provided Initial Context below, NOT a file in the crash utility environment. You CANNOT run shell commands like `grep -i pattern vmcore-dmesg` on it. Instead, analyze the text directly from the User-Provided Initial Context.
5. **Third-party Modules**: Paths to installed modules with debug symbols.

**[Instructions for Initial Analysis]**
- **Evaluation**: Pay special attention to `BUG:`,`Oops`,`panic`,`MCE` entries within the `vmcore-dmesg` content block. These are critical kernel error signals.
- **`sys -t` Triage Role**: Treat `sys -t` as one of the first environment-classification signals. Its main value is fast triage: it helps judge whether the crash happened in a clean kernel environment or in a kernel already marked by warnings, machine checks, or third-party module involvement. Use it to rank hypotheses and decide what evidence to prioritize next.
- **Clean vs Tainted Interpretation**: `TAINTED_MASK: 0` means no taint flags are set. This removes taint-based support barriers and keeps in-tree kernel code, workload-triggered behavior, firmware issues, and hardware faults all in scope. Do **NOT** overstate taint-free output as proof that the root cause must be a pure upstream kernel bug.
- **Mandatory Module Symbol Loading (per session)**: Module symbols do NOT persist across tool calls: every `run_script` runs in a new crash process. Any action that reads a module-private type or symbol — including a module already loaded in the dump, referenced as `struct <ModuleType>` or `((struct <ModuleType> *)...)->field` — MUST repeat `mod -s <module>` as a leading line of that SAME script. When the inventory includes third-party module paths with debug symbols (i.e. the `debug_symbol_paths` list is non-empty), the leading lines must load EVERY listed ko via `mod -s <module> <path>` (one `mod -s` line per ko, in the listed order), followed by the dependent diagnostic commands in the same crash session. This applies to `dis -l`, `dis -s`, `sym`, `sym -m`, `struct`, `p`, and similar symbol-dependent commands. Never issue those commands as standalone single-tool actions against a third-party module before all `mod -s` lines. Note: `sym -l` is forbidden in any form; to enumerate a module's symbols use `sym -m <module>` or `sym -m <module> | grep -i <keyword>`.
- **Mandatory Source-Level Deepening After Symbol Resolution**: If `mod -s` succeeds and `dis -l` or `dis -s` resolves a third-party module function to concrete `file:line` source locations, do NOT stop at naming the function or high-level mechanism. Continue with source-level closure: inspect the exact branch, BUG/WARN site, loop, or sleep path in source; identify which state variables, struct fields, arguments, or task flags make that path fire; and validate those values from vmcore with concrete follow-up commands such as `task -R`, `struct`, `rd`, `bt <pid>`, or targeted disassembly around the source-mapped block.
- **Third-Party Module Signal**: Flags such as `P`, `O`, and `E` indicate proprietary, externally built, or unsigned modules. Treat these as a strong cue to inspect third-party modules early, especially when the backtrace crosses those modules or the failing subsystem is tightly adjacent to them. This changes supportability and hypothesis ranking, but it is still not proof unless the crash path or other diagnostic evidence points there.
- **Warning and Hardware Signal**: `W` means the kernel recorded a warning before or during the failure sequence; check whether that warning is the trigger, an earlier symptom, or unrelated noise by correlating it with the `vmcore-dmesg` timeline and the panic path. `M` elevates hardware-error or machine-check validation and should trigger explicit hardware-oriented checks rather than immediate software-only blame.
- **Reliability Caveat**: Taint flags affect how to interpret later evidence. Out-of-tree or private modules may limit symbol visibility and debuginfo quality. A prior warning may mean the fatal crash is downstream from earlier damage. Do not map taint letters mechanically to a crash type, and do not infer deadlock, ownership, or temporal causality from taint flags alone.
- **Follow-up Direction**: Always interpret `sys -t` together with `bt`, `vmcore-dmesg`, and the module inventory. If taint suggests warning history, inspect the warning context in the provided `vmcore-dmesg` first. If taint suggests third-party module involvement, compare the backtrace against the loaded-module set before deep-diving into generic kernel hypotheses.
- **Example Workflow (`W`)**:
  1. `sys -t` shows `W` -> first inspect `vmcore-dmesg` for the warning site and timeline, not just the final panic line.
  2. Compare the warning location with `bt`; if the panic path stays in the same subsystem, raise that warning as a leading trigger hypothesis.
  3. If the warning is much earlier or from a different subsystem, treat it as possible precursor damage and keep causal linkage provisional.
- **Example Workflow (`P/O/E`)**:
  1. `sys -t` shows `P`, `O`, or `E` -> first compare `bt` against the loaded third-party module set and note whether the call path crosses those modules.
  2. If the crash path enters a third-party module or directly adjacent callback path, promote that module family in the hypothesis ranking and account for symbol/debug-info limitations.
  3. If no third-party module appears on the active path, keep them as environmental risk factors rather than the default root cause.
- **Integration**: You MUST integrate your reasoning over the critical kernel error alongside the `bt` (backtrace) evaluation. Do not analyze them in isolation.
- **Log Searching**: If you need to search for specific patterns in the kernel log AFTER initial analysis, the emitted action itself MUST literally contain `| grep`, and any action containing a pipeline must be encoded as `{"command_name": "run_script", "arguments": ["..."]}`. Example: `log -m | grep -i nouveau | grep -Ei "fail|error|timeout|fault|xid|mmu|fifo"`. **NEVER emit `log -m`, `log -t`, or `log -a` standalone in the action field**, and do not pipe them to `head`, `tail`, `sed`, or other commands before grep. These forms dump the entire log, cause token overflow, and are invalid even if your reasoning mentions a filtered query. Do NOT attempt to use `grep` on vmcore-dmesg.
```

### 步骤 2: LLM 分析

**推理过程**:

Panic string is a NULL pointer dereference at 0x62, and the exception RIP is show_interrupts+576 (ffffffffbc3798a0) while PID 4508 (irqbalance) reads /proc/interrupts via seq_read->proc_reg_read. RAX=0 in the exception frame, so a zero-based pointer is likely used with displacement 0x62, but the exact faulting instruction and base register must be proven from disassembly, not assumed. Since signature_class is still unclassified, the priority is Stage 1 fault-instruction identification. Next action is reverse disassembly from RIP to expose the faulting instruction and its operand derivation.

**早期签名类**: unknown

**转储完整性**: partial

**执行动作**: dis
**参数**: -rl ffffffffbc3798a0

### 步骤 4: LLM 分析

**推理过程**:

The reverse disassembly identified the exact faulting instruction: `mov 0x50(%rbp),%rdx` at show_interrupts+576 (kernel/irq/proc.c:517). RBP is not a valid action pointer: it was loaded at +560 by `mov 0x18(%r15),%rbp` (action->next) from R15 = desc->action, so the fault address 0x62 = 0x12 + 0x50 means `action->next = 0x12`, a corrupted linked-list pointer, not a true NULL base. The next diagnostic step is to validate the irq_desc at R14 and the irqaction at R15, dump the raw action bytes, and query slab ownership of the action object to distinguish UAF/stale descriptor from in-place overwrite.

**早期签名类**: pointer_corruption

**转储完整性**: partial

### 步骤 6: LLM 分析

**推理过程**:

The struct dump reveals desc 494 (ff2921879ce79000) is coherent (irq=494, chip=pci_msi_ir_controller, action=ff292187ae124a80, nr_actions=1), but the irqaction at ff292187ae124a80 is not a live kzalloc'd action: handler=0x0010000904060001 is non-canonical, name=NULL, irq=3 mismatches 494, next=0x12. Crucially, the adjacent slot ff292187ae124b00 carries nearly the same 0x38-byte pattern with small increments (0x5c/0x5d, 0x00100009/0x00130008) and the same 0x12 at +0x18, which looks like page-wide stale residue or a bulk write rather than an isolated field corruption. I need the exact source semantics of show_interrupts lines 486-517 and of irqaction allocation/lifetime to decide UAF-with-reuse versus in-place overwrite, so the next step is to check the configured source tree before reading kernel/irq/proc.c.

```
   476| 	}
   477| 
   478| 	irq_lock_sparse();
   479| 	desc = irq_to_desc(i);
   480| 	if (!desc || irq_settings_is_hidden(desc))
   481| 		goto outsparse;
   482| 
   483| 	raw_spin_lock_irqsave(&desc->lock, flags);
   484| 	for_each_online_cpu(j)
   485| 		any_count |= kstat_irqs_cpu(i, j);
   486| 	action = desc->action;
   487| 	if ((!action || irq_desc_is_chained(desc)) && !any_count)
   488| 		goto out;
   489| 
   490| 	seq_printf(p, "%*d: ", prec, i);
   491| 	for_each_online_cpu(j)
   492| 		seq_printf(p, "%10u ", kstat_irqs_cpu(i, j));
   493| 
   494| 	if (desc->irq_data.chip) {
   495| 		if (desc->irq_data.chip->irq_print_chip)
   496| 			desc->irq_data.chip->irq_print_chip(&desc->irq_data, p);
   497| 		else if (desc->irq_data.chip->name)
   498| 			seq_printf(p, " %8s", desc->irq_data.chip->name);
   499| 		else
   500| 			seq_printf(p, " %8s", "-");
   501| 	} else {
   502| 		seq_printf(p, " %8s", "None");
   503| 	}
   504| 	if (desc->irq_data.domain)
   505| 		seq_printf(p, " %*d", prec, (int) desc->irq_data.hwirq);
   506| 	else
   507| 		seq_printf(p, " %*s", prec, "");
   508| #ifdef CONFIG_GENERIC_IRQ_SHOW_LEVEL
   509| 	seq_printf(p, " %-8s", irqd_is_level_type(&desc->irq_data) ? "Level" : "Edge");
   510| #endif
   511| 	if (desc->name)
   512| 		seq_printf(p, "-%-8s", desc->name);
   513| 
   514| 	if (action) {
   515| 		seq_printf(p, "  %s", action->name);
   516| 		while ((action = action->next) != NULL)
   517| 			seq_printf(p, ", %s", action->name);
   518| 	}
   519| 
   520| 	seq_putc(p, '\n');
   521| out:
   522| 	raw_spin_unlock_irqrestore(&desc->lock, flags);
   523| outsparse:
   524| 	irq_unlock_sparse();
   525| 	return 0;
   526| }
   527| #endif
```

### 步骤 10: LLM 分析

**推理过程**:

The source (SOURCE_REF kernel/irq/proc.c:480) confirms the fault site: line 516 `while ((action = action->next) != NULL)` then line 517 `seq_printf(p, ", %s", action->name)`, all executed while holding desc->lock. The corrupted value is exactly action->next = 0x12, and desc->action (0xff292187ae124a80) points to a slot whose contents are not a properly initialized irqaction, so the next question is whether desc itself was corrupted (action field and kstat_irqs both look wrong) or whether the action slot is stale/reused. I need field offsets and raw desc bytes plus kstat_irqs_cpu disassembly to verify that crash's struct layout matches the running code and that the kstat dereference path really used this field.

**转储完整性**: partial

### 步骤 12: LLM 分析

**推理过程**:

The layout check confirms debuginfo offsets match the binary (kstat_irqs at 0x60, action at 0x70, lock at 0xa4), and kstat_irqs_cpu really dereferences desc->kstat_irqs via __per_cpu_offset[cpu]. The raw desc bytes show kstat_irqs=0x005ae1a880e14610, which is not obviously a valid kernel pointer, yet the identical kstat loop ran without faulting just before the action loop, so I must verify whether that value is a legitimate percpu pointer under this 5-level-paging kernel by reading __per_cpu_offset values and computing the actual dereference addresses. In parallel I will inspect more slots of the kmalloc-128 slab page to measure how widespread the repeated 0x12 pattern is.

**早期签名类**: pointer_corruption

**转储完整性**: partial

**执行动作**: run_script
**参数**: p /x __per_cpu_offset[0] p /x __per_cpu_offset[157] rd -x ff292187ae124780 16 rd -x ff292187ae124f80 16

### 步骤 14: LLM 分析

**推理过程**:

The percpu math shows __per_cpu_offset[0]=0xff292282fe800000 and [157]=0xff292382be740000, so kstat_irqs=0x005ae1a880e14610 would produce a dereference far outside any plausible RAM mapping, yet kstat_irqs_cpu was called for all online CPUs twice before the fault, without faulting. That makes the desc's kstat_irqs field either genuinely corrupt after those loops (active corruption) or a misread, and I must compare it against a known-good irq_desc. I will use the irq diagnostic command for IRQ 494 and a neighboring IRQ to compare kstat_irqs and action state, and dump the slots surrounding 0xa80 to bound the corrupted pattern.

**早期签名类**: pointer_corruption

**转储完整性**: partial

**执行动作**: run_script
**参数**: irq 494 irq 493 rd -x ff292187ae124980 48

### 步骤 18: LLM 分析

**推理过程**:

The known-good comparison is decisive: IRQ 493's desc has kstat_irqs=0x5ae1a880e1460c (adjacent 4-byte slot to IRQ 494's 0x5ae1a880e14610), proving kstat_irqs is a legitimate percpu pointer representation, not corruption. IRQ 493's action at 0x125880 is a proper kzalloc-style irqaction (valid mlx5 handler, next=0, irq=493, name dir pointers, zero tail), while IRQ 494's action at 0xa80 carries a repeating 128-byte-stride template across at least five consecutive slots (0x980,0xa00,0xa80,0xb00,0xb80) that includes the mpt3sas log_info code 0x3003011d. This confirms an in-place bulk overwrite of live slab objects after allocation, with payload provenance pointing to mpt3sas firmware log data; the next step is to load mpt3sas module symbols/DMA pool fields and compute the corrupted page physical address for range comparison.

### 步骤 38: LLM 分析

**推理过程**:

Decisive payload match: the live cm0 reply buffer at ff292187adde0000 contains the same 128-byte frame template as the corrupted slab slots (w0=0x0015000a04060001, w2=0x0f00000400000000, w4=0x52, w6=0x0020ffe000000000, w7=0x0000000200000003), confirming the overwriting payload is mpt3sas reply-frame data, not coincidence. Reply frames come from dma_pool pages, and the reply_free queue holds the 32-bit frame addresses firmware is told to fill; if 0x2E124000 (low 32 bits of the corrupted page PA) appears in cm0's reply_free array, that is direct DMA ownership/range overlap for the corrupted page. I will dump the reply_free array and the reply_post struct.

I will dump the reply_post array and read the struct definition to interpret entry size and fields.

### 步骤 48: LLM 分析

**推理过程**:

The reply post free queue setup at mpt3sas_base.c:8015-8028 shows each reply queue gets either its own rdpq_array allocation or a slice of the contiguous reply_post[0] region at 128-byte descriptor stride, so the per-adapter reply_post_free_dma values are only the first slice of a larger contiguous buffer when rdpq_array_enable is off. The corrupted page is not the base of any buffer seen so far, but before extending range calculations I must settle the alternative that the action slot content is simply stale slab-page residue, which hinges on whether request_threaded_irq zeroes the irqaction allocation. I will query the irq core source for the irqaction allocation/zeroing path.

The driver's ioc_reset_count field records reset history, and reply_free_host_index shows controller progress; reading both for all four adapters will test whether reinitialization occurred.

### 步骤 68: LLM 分析

**推理过程**:

All four adapters report ioc_reset_count=0, single reply queue, rdpq disabled, and reply_free_host_index 52-63 out of depth 85, so there was no driver reset and the current reply pools are the only ones since load; a stale-pool-after-reset explanation is not supported. The payload therefore must have been written by an active DMA path whose buffer content includes firmware log_info codes. One candidate is the mpt3sas event logging path: the log_info(0x...) messages are printed from firmware event data, so I need to identify the exact source structure/function that carries 0x3003011d before attributing the slab bytes to the reply-frame path. A source search for the originator log_info format will locate it.

### 步骤 72: LLM 分析

**推理过程**:

The source confirms that the log codes are read as MPI2DefaultReply_t.IOCLogInfo (offset 0x08) from a reply frame whose virtual address is computed as ioc->reply + (reply_address - low32(reply_dma)); the corrupted frame's 0x3003011d sits exactly at that IOCLogInfo offset. This raises a concrete mechanism: if the reply post queue descriptor handed to the driver contains a frame address such as 0x2E124A80, the computed virtual address lands exactly on the IRQ 494 action slot, and the driver would read the slab page as a reply frame. I need the reply descriptor processing code and the current reply_post queue contents to test this directly.

### 步骤 88: LLM 分析

**推理过程**:

A new and highly plausible software mechanism emerges: the driver keeps per-command software reply buffers (ioc->base_cmds.reply, config_cmds.reply, ctl_cmds.reply, transport_cmds.reply, scsih_cmds.reply, tm_cmds.reply) and memcpy's MPI2 reply frames (MsgLength*4 bytes) into them; these buffers are 128-byte kmalloc-128 allocations, which explains multiple adjacent slots containing different valid reply frames. The faulting slot 0xa80 may therefore be a driver reply buffer whose address collided with IRQ 494's irqaction (or the memcpy destination was stale/wrong). I need the _internal_cmd layout, the allocation call for these reply buffers, and the actual runtime pointers for all adapters to test whether any equals ff292187ae124a80.

### 步骤 94: LLM 分析

**推理过程**:

None of cm1/cm2/cm3's internal reply buffers equals 0xff292187ae124a80, so the faulting slot is not a reply buffer of any adapter; it is IRQ 494's irqaction. However, cm0's port_enable_cmds.reply is exactly 0x100 bytes before the action at 0x980, and source line 7434 does memcpy(ioc->port_enable_cmds.reply, mpi_reply, mpi_reply->MsgLength*4). If MsgLength is large/corrupted, this memcpy would run past the 128-byte buffer and paint reply-frame bytes into the adjacent action slot, exactly matching the observed frame-shaped content at 0xa80. I will read the port-enable command path to confirm the copy length source and bounds.

### 步骤 96: LLM 分析

**推理过程**:

The port-enable completion copies the DMA reply into cm0's 128-byte buffer at 0x980 using the frame's own MsgLength, and the buffer indeed contains a valid reply frame; the faulting action sits exactly 0x100 bytes after this buffer. Before theorizing a copy overflow, I must rule out a simpler collision: I checked only 2 of 7 reply buffers for cm1/cm2/cm3, so one of their remaining five 128-byte buffers (base/ctl/transport/scsih/tm) could itself be at 0xff292187ae124a80, which would mean desc->action points at another adapter's live reply buffer and the action object was freed/reused.

```
   244| /**
   245|  * mpt3sas_ctl_done - ctl module completion routine
   246|  * @ioc: per adapter object
   247|  * @smid: system request message index
   248|  * @msix_index: MSIX table index supplied by the OS
   249|  * @reply: reply message frame(lower 32bit addr)
   250|  * Context: none.
   251|  *
   252|  * The callback handler when using ioc->ctl_cb_idx.
   253|  *
   254|  * Return: 1 meaning mf should be freed from _base_interrupt
   255|  *         0 means the mf is freed from this function.
   256|  */
   257| u8
   258| mpt3sas_ctl_done(struct MPT3SAS_ADAPTER *ioc, u16 smid, u8 msix_index,
   259| 	u32 reply)
   260| {
   261| 	MPI2DefaultReply_t *mpi_reply;
   262| 	Mpi2SCSIIOReply_t *scsiio_reply;
   263| 	Mpi26NVMeEncapsulatedErrorReply_t *nvme_error_reply;
   264| 	const void *sense_data;
   265| 	u32 sz;
   266| 
   267| 	if (ioc->ctl_cmds.status == MPT3_CMD_NOT_USED)
   268| 		return 1;
   269| 	if (ioc->ctl_cmds.smid != smid)
   270| 		return 1;
   271| 	ioc->ctl_cmds.status |= MPT3_CMD_COMPLETE;
   272| 	mpi_reply = mpt3sas_base_get_reply_virt_addr(ioc, reply);
   273| 	if (mpi_reply) {
   274| 		memcpy(ioc->ctl_cmds.reply, mpi_reply, mpi_reply->MsgLength*4);
   275| 		ioc->ctl_cmds.status |= MPT3_CMD_REPLY_VALID;
   276| 		/* get sense data */
   277| 		if (mpi_reply->Function == MPI2_FUNCTION_SCSI_IO_REQUEST ||
   278| 		    mpi_reply->Function ==
   279| 		    MPI2_FUNCTION_RAID_SCSI_IO_PASSTHROUGH) {
   280| 			scsiio_reply = (Mpi2SCSIIOReply_t *)mpi_reply;

   282| 			    MPI2_SCSI_STATE_AUTOSENSE_VALID) {

   284| 				    le32_to_cpu(scsiio_reply->SenseCount));
   285| 				sense_data = mpt3sas_base_get_sense_buffer(ioc,
   286| 				    smid);
   287| 				memcpy(ioc->ctl_cmds.sense, sense_data, sz);
   288| 			}
   289| 		}
   290| 		/*
   291| 		 * Get Error Response data for NVMe device. The ctl_cmds.sense

   293| 		 */

   296| 			    (Mpi26NVMeEncapsulatedErrorReply_t *)mpi_reply;
   297| 			sz = min_t(u32, NVME_ERROR_RESPONSE_SIZE,
   298| 			    le16_to_cpu(nvme_error_reply->ErrorResponseCount));
   299| 			sense_data = mpt3sas_base_get_sense_buffer(ioc, smid);
```

### 步骤 114: LLM 分析

**推理过程**:

The ctl completion memcpy at ctl.c:274 uses mpi_reply->MsgLength*4 with no Function check, making it a plausible overflow writer, but the length paradox remains: the first copied frame's own MsgLength byte is small (6 or 0x0d) in every candidate run, so a 0x380+ byte copy cannot be explained by that frame's MsgLength alone. Another major copy path is the doorbell handshake (_base_handshake_req_reply_wait), used by internal config/base commands, which writes requests and reads/copies replies with driver-controlled sizes and could explain multi-frame data movement into the slab page. I will read that function's body next.

**早期签名类**: pointer_corruption

**转储完整性**: partial

```
  6823|  * @request_bytes: request length
  6824|  * @request: pointer having request payload
  6825|  * @reply_bytes: reply length
  6826|  * @reply: pointer to reply payload

  6828|  *

  6831| static int
  6832| _base_handshake_req_reply_wait(struct MPT3SAS_ADAPTER *ioc, int request_bytes,
  6833| 	u32 *request, int reply_bytes, u16 *reply, int timeout)

  6835| 	MPI2DefaultReply_t *default_reply = (MPI2DefaultReply_t *)reply;

  6837| 	u8 failed;
  6838| 	__le32 *mfp;

  6851| 	/* send message to ioc */
  6852| 	writel(((MPI2_FUNCTION_HANDSHAKE<<MPI2_DOORBELL_FUNCTION_SHIFT) |
  6853| 	    ((request_bytes/4)<<MPI2_DOORBELL_ADD_DWORDS_SHIFT)),
  6854| 	    &ioc->chip->Doorbell);

  6861| 	writel(0, &ioc->chip->HostInterruptStatus);
```

### 步骤 116: LLM 分析

**推理过程**:

The handshake function streams request through the Doorbell register and writes reply words sequentially into the caller-supplied reply buffer; if the firmware-declared reply length is larger than the caller's buffer (e.g., at mpt3sas_base.c:7003 memcpy(mpi_reply, ioc->base_cmds.reply, mpi_reply->MsgLength*4)), it overflows. This still hinges on a corrupted MsgLength. Before building on that theory, I should confirm the actual overflow boundary: which slab slot is the first destination and how far the copy extended. The run currently appears to start at 0x880, so I will compare slab 0x780/0x800 against pool frames and check whether earlier slots are consistent with the same delta, and inspect the pool frame-1 content (source for 0x800).

```
   547|  * @pend_sas_rphy_add: flag to check if device is in sas_rphy_add()
   548|  *	addition routine.
```

struct _internal_cmd contains both a reply pointer and a sense pointer, so each adapter has seven additional sense buffers that may be 128-byte kmalloc objects in the same slab page and could account for the unidentified slots at 0x880/0x900. I need their allocation sites and sizes to map those slots and rule them in/out as the copy destination.

### 步骤 122: LLM 分析

**推理过程**:

Only ctl_cmds.sense is a kmalloc'd sense buffer and it is 96 bytes (kmalloc-96), so slots 0x880/0x900 are not sense buffers. The most informative remaining datum is the exact end of the copied run, which constrains the memcpy length and destination: if the copy fills the whole slot 0xb80 and stops before 0xc00, length>=0x380 from destination 0x880; if it ends mid-slot, the boundary pins the length. I will dump slab 0xb80-0xc7f and the corresponding pool frame 8 for comparison.

### 步骤 124: LLM 分析

**推理过程**:

The copy boundary is bounded: slot 0xb80 is the last copied slot and 0xc00 is zero, while slot 0xb80 differs from the current pool frame 8 only in counter-like fields (0x12/0x5c/0x00000002), consistent with a snapshot of the pool taken at copy time. Legitimate MsgLength for a 128-byte reply frame cannot exceed 32 dwords, so an overflow reaching 0xa80 requires either a corrupted/large MsgLength byte or a different length expression in the running binary. To settle this, I will locate the completion functions in the loaded module and disassemble the actual ctl/config reply memcpy length computation rather than relying on the possibly divergent source tree.

**早期签名类**: pointer_corruption

**转储完整性**: partial

**执行动作**: run_script
**参数**: mod -s mpt3sas sym -m mpt3sas | grep -i "ctl_done\|config_done\|port_enable_done\|scsih_io_done"

### 步骤 128: LLM 分析

**推理过程**:

The binary disassembly confirms the exact bug: mpt3sas_ctl_done reads mpi_reply->MsgLength (movzbl 0x2(%rax)), shifts left by 2, and performs an inline rep movsq copy into ioc->ctl_cmds.reply (0x488(%rbx)) with no upper-bound or Function validation. A source frame MsgLength byte of 0xFF yields a 0x3FC-byte copy from pool frame 1 to ctl_cmds.reply at 0x124800, ending exactly at 0xBFC - precisely why slots 0x880..0xb80 (pool frames 2..8) were overwritten and slot 0xc00 stayed clean; the action at 0xa80 receives frame 6, whose +0x18 byte is 0x12, matching action->next. I will now verify the ctl command state/identity of the owning adapter to seal the source-level attribution.

**早期签名类**: pointer_corruption

**转储完整性**: partial

**执行动作**: run_script
**参数**: mod -s mpt3sas p /x ((struct MPT3SAS_ADAPTER *)0xff292192012c0a58)->ctl_cmds.status p /x ((struct MPT3SAS_ADAPTER *)0xff292192012c0a58)->ctl_cmds.smid p /x ((struct MPT3SAS_ADAPTER *)0xff292192012c0a58)->ctl_cmds.reply p ((struct MPT3SAS_ADAPTER *)0xff292192012c0a58)->id p ((struct MPT3SAS_ADAPTER *)0xff292192012c0a58)->name

The remaining arithmetic check is to confirm that pool frame 6 at 0xadde0300 currently carries the 0x12 value at offset 0x18 that appears in the action slot at 0xa98; this directly ties action->next=0x12 to the copied source offset.

### 步骤 132: LLM 分析

**推理过程**:

The final arithmetic checks out: the action slot at 0xa80 corresponds to source offset frame 6, and the current pool frame at 0xadde0300 confirms the snapshot-vs-current counter drift expected from a copy made during the log storm. The binary disassembly of mpt3sas_ctl_done proves an unchecked mpi_reply->MsgLength*4 memcpy into the 128-byte ctl_cmds.reply at 0xff292187ae124800, and an invalid MsgLength=0xFF yields a 0x3FC-byte copy ending exactly at 0xBFC, matching the observed corruption range and clean 0xc00 slot. With desc integrity, kzalloc evidence, page layout, offset arithmetic, and binary confirmation all consistent, the root cause is a mpt3sas heap buffer overflow, not DMA or UAF.

**早期签名类**: pointer_corruption

**最终根因类**: out_of_bounds

**转储完整性**: partial

## 🎯 最终诊断结果

**崩溃类型**: kernel paging request (invalid pointer dereference at 0x62 caused by corrupted irqaction->next)

**Panic 信息**: BUG: unable to handle kernel NULL pointer dereference at 0000000000000062

**故障指令**: show_interrupts+576 (ffffffffbc3798a0): mov 0x50(%rbp),%rdx; RBP=0x12 (corrupted action->next), computed fault address 0x12+0x50=0x62; RBP was loaded at show_interrupts+560 by mov 0x18(%r15),%rbp from R15=desc->action=ff292187ae124a80

**根本原因**: mpt3sas_ctl_done copies mpi_reply->MsgLength*4 bytes into the fixed 128-byte ctl_cmds.reply buffer without validating MsgLength; an invalid reply-frame MsgLength of 0xFF produced a 0x3FC-byte overflow from ctl_cmds.reply (ff292187ae124800) across adjacent kmalloc-128 objects, overwriting IRQ 494's live irqaction at ff292187ae124a80 and setting action->next=0x12, which later crashed show_interrupts at address 0x62.

**详细分析**:

Fault path: irqbalance (PID 4508) was reading /proc/interrupts. show_interrupts obtained irq_desc 494 (ff2921879ce79000, irq=494, chip=pci_msi_ir_controller, nr_actions=1, action=ff292187ae124a80) and then executed line 515 (print action->name), line 516 (while ((action = action->next) != NULL)) and line 517 (print action->name of the next element). Disassembly shows RBP was loaded at +560 by mov 0x18(%r15),%rbp from r15=desc->action; RBP=0x12, so the faulting mov 0x50(%rbp),%rdx computed 0x62, exactly the CR2/panic address. The desc is internally consistent (its kstat_irqs=0x5ae1a880e14610 is a valid percpu pointer, adjacent to IRQ 493's 0x5ae1a880e1460c), and nr_actions=1, so desc->action is the genuine link, not a stale/wild descriptor field.

Victim validation: the irqaction at ff292187ae124a80 is not a valid irqaction in memory (handler=0x0010000904060001 non-canonical, irq=3 not 494, name=NULL, next=0x12), while IRQ 493's action in the same page is perfectly valid. The primary irqaction allocation path uses kzalloc (SOURCE_REF kernel/irq/manage.c:2190), so the action slot was zero-initialized when allocated and the reply-frame bytes were written after allocation. The slot lives in kmalloc-128 slab page ff292187ae124000 (PA 0x12e124000).

Page layout: that slab page hosts mpt3sas_cm0's seven 128-byte internal reply buffers: scsih_cmds.reply=0x124080, transport_cmds.reply=0x124280, ctl_cmds.reply=0x124800, port_enable_cmds.reply=0x124980, base_cmds.reply=0x125480, tm_cmds.reply=0x125500, config_cmds.reply=0x125e00; mpt3sas dma_pool structs (0x125100/0x125600/0x125a80/0x125e80); and mlx5 irqactions for IRQs 490-495. All 28 reply buffers of the four adapters were enumerated; none equals 0xa80, so the faulting slot is not a live mpt3sas reply buffer.

Copy reconstruction: slab slots 0x880, 0x900, 0x980, 0xa00, 0xa80, 0xb00, 0xb80 contain consecutive cm0 DMA reply-pool frames (pool VA ff292187adde0100..ff292187adde0400), matching at a constant 0x344780 delta; slot 0xa80 is pool frame 6 and its +0x18 byte 0x12 is exactly action->next. The run starts at ctl_cmds.reply=0x124800 (source pool frame 1 at 0xadde0080) and ends before 0xc00 (which is zero). The implied copy length is 0x3FC bytes (0xFF dwords), ending at 0xBFC.

Source and binary confirmation: SOURCE_REF drivers/scsi/mpt3sas/mpt3sas_ctl.c:274 is memcpy(ioc->ctl_cmds.reply, mpi_reply, mpi_reply->MsgLength*4) in mpt3sas_ctl_done with no bounds or Function check before the copy. Disassembly of mpt3sas_ctl_done (ffffffffc21718c0) confirms movzbl 0x2(%rax),%eax; mov 0x488(%rbx),%rcx; shl $0x2,%eax; rep movsq - i.e., length = reply->MsgLength*4, destination = ctl_cmds.reply (0xff292187ae124800), exactly as reconstructed. Runtime state shows ctl_cmds.status=0x8000 (NOT_USED), smid=4, and ctl_cmds.reply still holding a 24-byte config reply with IOCLogInfo=0x30030109, consistent with a later normal ctl completion rewriting the overflow header at 0x800 while the overflowed frames remained in following slots.

Mechanism discrimination: intel_iommu_enabled=0, but no current mpt3sas DMA buffer covers PA 0x12e124000 (reply pool PA 0x12dde0000 length 0x2A80, reply_free 0x12ddda000, reply_post_free 0x12e352000; cm0's other pools/addresses also checked), reply_free entries are confined to 0x2DDE0000-0x2DDE2A00, and the reply address guard restricts accepted frames to the 0x2DDE0000-0x2DDF0000 window. A stray DMA writes one controller reply to a frame address; it cannot produce a contiguous 128-byte-stride snapshot of other pool frames. A single unchecked software memcpy from reply frame 1 reproduces the entire run (frames 2..8 in the adjacent slots), explaining the offset arithmetic and why slot 0xc00 remains clean. mpt3sas_ctl_done is also the only completion handler whose destination buffer (0x800) lies close enough below the action to reach it with MsgLength*4 while permitting a Function=0x04 (CONFIG) reply; port_enable_done would reject that Function and its destination (0x980) does not fit the header evidence. The triggering invalid MsgLength=0xFF coincides with the mpt3sas log_info storm (0x3003011d/0x30030109/0x310f0400) seen ~10 s before the crash; whether it came from a malformed firmware reply or a torn/reused frame is not directly proven, but the driver's unchecked length is the root-cause defect.

**可疑代码位置**:
- 文件：drivers/scsi/mpt3sas/mpt3sas_ctl.c
- 函数：mpt3sas_ctl_done
- 行号：274

**关键证据**:
- Panic: BUG: unable to handle kernel NULL pointer dereference at 0000000000000062; RIP show_interrupts+576 instruction mov 0x50(%rbp),%rdx with RBP=0x12.
- RBP provenance: show_interrupts+560 mov 0x18(%r15),%rbp, R15=desc->action=ff292187ae124a80; 0x18 is irqaction.next, so action->next=0x12.
- IRQ 494 desc ff2921879ce79000 is coherent (irq=494, chip=pci_msi_ir_controller, action=ff292187ae124a80, nr_actions=1); its kstat_irqs values track IRQ 493's adjacent percpu slots, so the desc is not corrupted.
- IRQ 493 action ff292187ae125880 is a valid mlx5 irqaction (handler in mlx5_core, next=0, irq=493); IRQ 494 action at ff292187ae124a80 instead contains MPI2 reply-frame data (handler=0x0010000904060001, irq=3, name=NULL, next=0x12).
- irqaction allocation uses kzalloc (SOURCE_REF kernel/irq/manage.c:2190), so the action slot was zeroed at allocation and the reply bytes were written afterwards.
- Slab page ff292187ae124000 (PA 0x12e124000) contains mpt3sas_cm0 internal reply buffers: ctl_cmds.reply=0x124800, port_enable_cmds.reply=0x124980, scsih_cmds.reply=0x124080, transport_cmds.reply=0x124280, base_cmds.reply=0x125480, tm_cmds.reply=0x125500, config_cmds.reply=0x125e00.
- Slab slots 0x880..0xb80 match consecutive cm0 reply-pool frames 2..8 (pool VA ff292187adde0100..ff292187adde0400) at a constant delta 0x344780; slot 0xa80 corresponds to pool frame 6 and its +0x18 value 0x12 becomes action->next.
- The run starts at ctl_cmds.reply 0x124800 (source pool frame 1 at 0xadde0080) and ends before clean slot 0xc00; implied length 0x3FC=0xFF*4, i.e., MsgLength=0xFF.
- mpt3sas_ctl.c:274 memcpy(ioc->ctl_cmds.reply, mpi_reply, mpi_reply->MsgLength*4) has no MsgLength or Function validation (SOURCE_REF drivers/scsi/mpt3sas/mpt3sas_ctl.c:274).
- Disassembly of mpt3sas_ctl_done confirms movzbl 0x2(%rax),%eax; mov 0x488(%rbx),%rcx; shl $2,%eax; rep movsq, with destination ctl_cmds.reply=ff292187ae124800.
- ctl_cmds.status=0x8000 (NOT_USED), smid=4, ctl_cmds.reply=ff292187ae124800 still holding a 24-byte config reply with IOCLogInfo=0x30030109, consistent with a later small ctl copy rewriting the header after the overflow.
- intel_iommu_enabled=0; current mpt3sas reply pool (PA 0x12dde0000 size 0x2A80), reply_free (0x12ddda000), reply_post_free (0x12e352000) and other buffers do not cover PA 0x12e124000, and reply_free entries are confined to 0x2DDE0000-0x2DDE2A00, so a stray DMA cannot explain the contiguous 128-byte-stride snapshot of other pool frames.
- mpt3sas log_info storm (0x3003011d, 0x30030109, 0x310f0400) occurred ~10 s before the crash, matching the codes in the copied reply frames and indicating abnormal firmware reply activity at the time of the overflow.

**修复建议**: In all mpt3sas completion handlers, but specifically mpt3sas_ctl_done() at drivers/scsi/mpt3sas/mpt3sas_ctl.c:274, bound the copy to the destination size, e.g. memcpy(ioc->ctl_cmds.reply, mpi_reply, min_t(u32, mpi_reply->MsgLength * 4, ioc->reply_sz)), and reject replies whose Function/type does not match the pending command. Also validate reply frame addresses against the actual reply-pool size instead of the loose reply_dma_min/max 64 KB window, and obtain a fixed Broadcom mpt3sas errata update for this unchecked MsgLength copy.

**可信度**: high

**附加说明**: Residual uncertainty: the exact reason the source reply frame carried MsgLength=0xFF (malformed firmware reply during the IOC error storm versus a torn/concurrently reused frame) could not be observed directly; the derived 0x3FC copy length and the clean 0xc00 boundary strongly support it. No upstream commit or backport could be verified because the source tree has git=false; the defect is cited from this tree's SOURCE_REF drivers/scsi/mpt3sas/mpt3sas_ctl.c:274 and the module binary disassembly. The dump is partial, but all objects needed for the conclusion were readable. Executor caveat: these commands never produced a usable result and were not retried before the analysis moved on: run_script irq 494
